On the Fourier analysis of the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system: Growth and Decay of the Fourier constants
Athanasios Chatzikaleas

Abstract
. We consider the ( 1 + 3 ) (1+3) -dimensional Einstein equations with negative cosmological constant coupled to a spherically-symmetric, massless scalar field and study perturbations around the Anti-de Sitter spacetime. We derive the resonant systems, pick out vanishing secular terms and discuss issues related to small divisors. Most importantly, we rigorously establish (sharp, in most of the cases) asymptotic behaviour for all the interaction coefficients. The latter is based on uniform estimates for the eigenfunctions associated to the linearized operator as well as on some oscillatory integrals.



1.
Introduction

In this work, we initiate a rigorous study of the dynamics in Fourier space
of spherically symmetric solutions to the coupled Einstein-Klein-Gordon system,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\text{Ric}(g)-\frac{1}{2}g\text{R}(g)+\Lambda g=8\pi\left(d\phi\otimes d\phi-\frac{1}{2}g|d\phi|_{g}^{2}\right),\quad\Lambda<0,\\
\Box_{g}\phi=0,\end{cases}

(1.1)

around the Anti-de Sitter solution. Here,
\text{Ric}(g)
and
R(g)
denote the Ricci and scalar curvatures respectively, both with respect to the spacetime metric
g
,
\Box_{g}
stands for the wave operator and
\Lambda
is the cosmological constant.

1.1.
(In-)stability of Anti-de Sitter

The Ant-de Sitter (AdS) spacetime is the unique (up to quotients by isometry subgroups) maximally symmetric solution to the Einstein equations with negative cosmological constant,

\displaystyle\text{Ric}(g)=-\Lambda g,\quad\Lambda<0.

In local compactified coordinates
(t,x,\omega)\in\mathcal{M}:=\mathbb{R}\times\left[0,\pi/2\right)\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, it reads

\displaystyle g_{\text{AdS}}(t,x,\omega)=\frac{1}{\cos^{2}(x)}\left(-dt^{2}+dx^{2}+\sin^{2}(x)d\omega^{2}\right)

implying that
(\mathcal{M},g_{\text{AdS}})
is conformal to half of the Einstein static universe. One can see that, altough the spatial distance from any point
(t,x)
with
0\leq x<\pi/2
to
\mathcal{I}:=\{x=\pi/2\}
is infinite, null geodesics reach the conformal spatial infinity
\mathcal{I}
in finite time with respect to time as measured by an inertial observer at the center. The particular characteristic of the AdS solution as well as of all asymptotically AdS spacetimes (that are spacetimes that approach the AdS solution at infinity in the spatial directions fast enough and share the same conformal boundary) is that the conformal spatial infinity is a time-like cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Consequently, the AdS metric is not globally hyperbolic and hence in order to study the evolution of the field
\phi
on such backgrounds one has to prescribe boundary conditions also on
\mathcal{I}
in addition to the initial data on the
\{t=0\}
slice.

It is well known that the Minkowski space is a ground state among asymptotically flat spacetimes
[
35
,
37
]
. The AdS spacetime also enjoys a similar variational characterization due to the positive energy theorem which states that for solutions to the general Einstein field equations which are globally regular and satisfy a reasonable energy condition, the AdS space is a ground state among asymptotically AdS spacetimes
[
24
]
. As far as the initial-boundary value problem is concerned, Smulevici-Holzegel
[
26
]
(for Dirichlet boundary conditions) and Warnick-Holzegel
[
27
]
(for more general boundary conditions) proved its local well-posedness in the class of spherically symmetric solutions. We also note that, if one removes the symmetry assumptions, Friedrich’s proof of local well-posedness for the vacuum equations yields local well-posedness for the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system provided that the latter has the conformal value of the mass. Once the local well-posedness is established, an important question for the AdS solution (as for any ground state) is whether it is stable or not, meaning whether small perturbations of the solution on the
\{t=0\}
slice remain small for all future times or not. For the Minkowski spacetime such a question has been answered by Christodoulou-Klainerman
[
16
]
and for the de-Sitter spacetime by Friedrich
[
23
]
who proved its stability.

The main mechanism responsible for the stability of the Minkowski spacetime is the dissipation of energy by dispersion. In the case of AdS solution, such a mechanism is no longer present. For “reflective” boundary conditions on the conformal infinity, waves which start at any point inside the region
\{0\leq x<\pi/2\}
and propagate outwards are reflected on
\mathcal{I}
and return back to into the region from where they started
[
12
]
. Such boundary conditions are confining enough forcing the AdS solution to act as a closed universe (in terms of its fields inside).

Although the conjecture on the instability of the AdS spacetime was first announced by Dafermos
[
19
]
and Dafermos-Holzegel
[
20
]
in 2006, the first work in this direction was the seminal paper of Bizoń-Rostworowski
[
11
]
. Specifically, Bizoń-Rostworowski
[
11
]
considered the spherically symmetric Einstein massless scalar field equations with negative cosmological constant in
(1+3)
-dimensions and established strong numerical (as well as analytical) results indicating that the AdS solution to the Einstein equations (although linearly stable) is in fact nonlinearly unstable against the formation of a black hole under arbitrarily small and generic perturbations. In their work
[
11
]
, they used specific Gaussian-type initial data and concluded that such initial data evolve to a wave which, as it propagates in time, collapses quickly and an apparent horizon appears. On top of their numerical findings, Bizoń-Rostworowski
[
11
]
also proposed the resonant mode mixing as the mechanism responsible for the AdS instability. The authors expanded the dynamical variables in terms of the eigenfunctions
\{e_{n}:n\geq 0\}
to the associated linearized operator. Subsequently, this resulted in the construction of a perturbative series expansion bifurcating from initial data dominated only by a fixed number of modes. Inserting this expansion in the non-linear system, one obtains an infinite system of harmonic oscillators in which secular
1
1

1

These are terms of the form
\tau^{\alpha}\sin(\beta\tau)
and
\tau^{\alpha}\cos(\beta\tau)
for some
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.
terms appear naturally, see also Section
3.2
. According to
[
11
]
, if these terms are not removed, they should eventually become responsible for exponential instability and a possible breakdown of the solutions. For example, if one starts with initial data dominated by the first 2-modes, namely
e_{0}+e_{1}
, then a secular term occurs that cannot be removed, for more details see
[
11
]
and page 20 in
[
34
]
. Their important work then triggered a substantial amount of numerical and heuristic studies. Dias-Horowitz-Santos
[
22
]
considered pure gravity with a negative cosmological constant and provided additional support strengthening the evidence that the AdS spacetime might be nonlinearly unstable. Moreover, similar results have been obtained by Jałmużna-Rostworowski-Bizoń
[
29
]
and Buchel-Lehner-Liebling
[
13
]
for higher dimensions as well as by Choptuik
[
15
]
who studied the mechanism of the spherically symmetric collapse of a scalar field with a general time and radial spatial dependent metric and for several families of initial data.

Most importantly, Bizoń-Rostworowski
[
11
]
also conjectured that there may exist specific initial data (called islands of stability) for which the evolution of small perturbations around the AdS solution remains globally regular in time. As a matter of fact, Maliborski-Rostworowski
[
30
]
considered the spherically symmetric Einstein-massless scalar field equations with negative cosmological constant in
1+d
dimensions with
d\geq 2
and provided reliable numerical evidence indicating that actually time-periodic solutions may exist for non-generic initial data. They were able to construct these solutions using both nonlinear perturbative expansions and fully nonlinear numerical methods. Specifically, they expanded the dynamical variables in terms of the eigenfunctions
\{e_{n}:n\geq 0\}
to the associated linearized operator and constructed a perturbative series expansion bifurcating from initial data dominated only by 1-modes. Then, they inserted this in the non-linear system and obtained a recurrence relation where one can fine tune the initial data order-by-order in such a way so that all secular terms appearing in the resonant system can be removed. Consequently, they are left with a secular free perturbative series that presumably converges obtaining a time-periodic solution from non-generic initial data. However, their analysis suggests that all secular terms can be removed provided that one can verify an infinite system of both linear and non-linear conditions where the so called Fourier coefficients play a decisive role in its validity. These are oscillatory integrals defined by the mode couplings induced by the non-linearities of the system and are essentially weighted integrals of products of the eigenfunctions
\{e_{n}:n\geq 0\}
to the associated linearized operator, see Sections
3.1
and
4.1
as well as
[
18
,
11
,
30
]
. In this work, we consider the coupled Einstein-Klein-Gordon system in spherical symmetry, track down various cancellations occurring in the potentially resonant terms and establish robust bounds for the Fourier coefficients using two types of perturbations.

Moreover, similar conjectures were made by Dias-Horowitz-Marolf-Santos
[
21
]
who argued that many asymptotically AdS solutions are nonlinearly stable (including geons, boson stars, and black holes) and by Buchel-Liebling-Lehner
[
14
]
who considered boson stars in global AdS spacetime and study their stability. Finally, a very interesting non-perturbative approach was developed by Moschidis who rigorously proved the AdS instability conjecture for generic initial data and for the Einstein–massless Vlasov system and Einstein–null dust system in his celebrated works
[
32
,
33
,
31
]
.

1.2.
Spherical symmetric ansatz

We consider the coupled Einstein-Klein-Gordon system (
1.1
) in spherical symmetry and for simplicity we fix the spatial dimension
d=3
. Following the work of Bizoń-Rostworowski
[
11
]
we parametrize the spacetime metric by the spherically symmetric ansatz

\displaystyle g(t,x,\omega)=\frac{1}{\cos^{2}(x)}\left(-A(t,x)e^{-2\delta(t,x)}dt^{2}+A^{-1}(t,x)dx^{2}+\sin^{2}(x)d\omega^{2}\right),

(1.2)

for
(t,x,\omega)\in\mathcal{M}
. Under this ansatz, the wave equation in spherical symmetry becomes

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(A^{-1}(t,x)e^{\delta(t,x)}\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)\right)=\frac{1}{\tan^{2}(x)}\partial_{x}\left(\tan^{2}(x)A(t,x)e^{-\delta(t,x)}\partial_{x}\phi(t,x)\right).

(1.3)

We note that the gauge condition used here includes the normalization
\delta=0
and
A=1
at
x=0
. We transform the second order partial differential equation (
1.3
) into a first order system by setting

\displaystyle\Phi(t,x)=\partial_{x}\phi(t,x),~~~\Pi(t,x)=\frac{1}{A(t,x)e^{-\delta(t,x)}}\partial_{t}\phi(t,x).

Then, (
1.3
) reads

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\Phi(t,x)=\partial_{x}\left(A(t,x)e^{-\delta(t,x)}\Pi(t,x)\right),\\
\partial_{t}\Pi(t,x)=-\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-3.40279pt\hbox{$L$}\hskip-3.40279pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{1.77776pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.59816pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 411.22394pt}\left(A(t,x)e^{-\delta(t,x)}\Phi(t,x)\right),\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-3.40279pt\hbox{$L$}\hskip-3.40279pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{1.77776pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.59816pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}[g](x):=-\frac{1}{\tan^{2}(x)}\partial_{x}(\tan^{2}(x)g(x)),

coupled to the Einstein equations

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(1-A(t,x))e^{-\delta(t,x)}=\frac{\cos^{3}(x)}{\sin(x)}\int_{0}^{x}e^{-\delta(t,y)}\left(\Phi^{2}(t,y)+\Pi^{2}(t,y)\right)(\tan(y))^{2}dy,\\
-\delta(t,x)=\int_{0}^{x}\left(\Phi^{2}(t,y)+\Pi^{2}(t,y)\right)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy.\end{cases}

1.3.
The linearized operator

From the Einstein equations, one can derive an additional equation, namely the momentum constraint

\displaystyle\partial_{t}A(t,x)=-2\sin(x)\cos(x)A(t,x)\partial_{x}\phi(t,x)\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)

and now (
1.3
) can be written as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}\phi(t,x)+L\left[\phi(t,x)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}\big(\big(A(t,x)e^{-\delta(t,x)}\big)^{2}\big)\partial_{x}\phi(t,x)+\big(1-\big(A(t,x)e^{-\delta(t,x)}\big)^{2}\big)L\left[\phi(t,x)\right]

\displaystyle-2\sin(x)\cos(x)\partial_{x}\phi(t,x)\left(\partial_{t}\phi(t,x)\right)^{2}-\partial_{t}\delta(t,x)\partial_{t}\phi(t,x),

where

\displaystyle L[f](x):=-\frac{1}{\tan^{2}(x)}\partial_{x}(\tan^{2}(x)\partial_{x}f(x))

is the operator which governs linearized perturbations of AdS solution. The solutions to the eigenvalue problem
L[f]=\omega^{2}f
subject to Dirichlet boundary conditions on the conformal boundary
\mathcal{I}=\{x=\pi/2\}

fall into the hypergeometric class and hence can be found explicitly. For a rigorous definition of the spectrum as well as for the definition of the domain in which the linearized operator is self-adjoint, see the work of Bachelot
[
1
]
. Specifically, the eigenvalues read

\displaystyle\omega^{2}_{j}:=(3+2j)^{2}

and eigenfunctions are weighted Jacobi polynomials,

\displaystyle e_{j}(x):=2\frac{\sqrt{j!(j+2)!}}{\Gamma(j+\frac{3}{2})}\cos^{3}(x)P^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}}_{j}(\cos(2x)),

for all integers
j=0,1,2,\dots
and
x\in[0,\pi/2]
. For the definition, basic properties and an introduction to the Jacobi polynomials see Chapter 4, page 48 in Szegö’s book
[
36
]
. In addition, the linearized operator
L
is self-adjoint with respect to the weighted inner product

\displaystyle(f|g):=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}f(x)g(x)\tan^{2}(x)dx.

(1.4)

Finally, note that the eigenvalues are strictly positive and hence the linear problem is orbitally stable.

2.
Main result and preliminaries

We consider the spherically symmetric Einstein-massless scalar field equations with negative cosmological constant under the spherically symmetric ansatz (
1.2
),

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Phi(t,x)=\partial_{x}\left(A(t,x)e^{-\delta(t,x)}\Pi(t,x)\right),

(2.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Pi(t,x)=-\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-3.40279pt\hbox{$L$}\hskip-3.40279pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{1.77776pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.59816pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94691pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.3611pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-165.94725pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.97221pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}\left(A(t,x)e^{-\delta(t,x)}\Phi(t,x)\right),

(2.2)

\displaystyle(1-A(t,x))e^{-\delta(t,x)}=\frac{\cos^{3}(x)}{\sin(x)}\int_{0}^{x}e^{-\delta(t,y)}\left(\Phi^{2}(t,y)+\Pi^{2}(t,y)\right)(\tan(y))^{2}dy,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\delta(t,x)=-\int_{0}^{x}\left(\Phi^{2}(t,y)+\Pi^{2}(t,y)\right)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy,

(2.4)

and we are mainly interested in the asymptotic behaviour of the Fourier constants which appear in the analysis of perturbations around the AdS solution
(\Phi,\Pi,A,\delta)=(0,0,1,0)
.

2.1.
Statement of the main result

Specifically, we consider two types of perturbations. On the one hand, in light of recent work Maliborski-Rostworowski
[
30
]
, although the series may not converge, we seek a solution of the form

\displaystyle\Phi(t,x)=\sum_{\lambda=0}^{\infty}\psi_{2\lambda+1}(\tau,x)\epsilon^{2\lambda+1},\quad\Pi(t,x)=\sum_{\lambda=0}^{\infty}\sigma_{2\lambda+1}(\tau,x)\epsilon^{2\lambda+1},

(2.5)

\displaystyle A(t,x)e^{-\delta(t,x)}=\sum_{\lambda=0}^{\infty}\xi_{2\lambda}(\tau,x)\epsilon^{2\lambda},\quad e^{-\delta(t,x)}=\sum_{\lambda=0}^{\infty}\zeta_{2\lambda}(\tau,x)\epsilon^{2\lambda},

(2.6)

\displaystyle\tau=\Omega_{\gamma}t,\quad\Omega_{\gamma}=\sum_{\lambda=0}^{\infty}\omega_{\gamma,2\lambda}\epsilon^{2\lambda},

(2.7)

where
\psi_{2\lambda+1}
,
\sigma_{2\lambda+1}
,
\xi_{2\lambda}
and
\zeta_{2\lambda}
are all periodic in time and in particular
\xi_{0}=\zeta_{0}=1
. Here,
\psi_{1},\sigma_{1},\xi_{2},\zeta_{2},\omega_{\gamma,0},\gamma
will be chosen later. Furthermore, with a slight abuse of notation, we use the same letters to denote the variables with respect to the
(\tau,x)
and
(t,x)
.
On the other hand, we still assume that
(\Phi,\Pi,A,\delta)
are all close to the AdS solution
(0,0,1,0)
but expand them using a finite sum

\displaystyle\Phi(t,x)=\Phi_{1}(\tau,x)\epsilon+\Psi(\tau,x)\epsilon^{3},\quad\Pi(t,x)=\Pi_{1}(\tau,x)\epsilon+\Sigma(\tau,x)\epsilon^{3}

(2.8)

\displaystyle A(t,x)=1-A_{2}(\tau,x)\epsilon^{2}-B(\tau,x)\epsilon^{4},\quad e^{-\delta(t,x)}=1-\delta_{2}(\tau,x)\epsilon^{2}-\Theta(\tau,x)\epsilon^{4},

(2.9)

\displaystyle\tau=(\omega_{\gamma}+\epsilon^{2}\theta_{\gamma}+\epsilon^{4}\eta_{\gamma})t,

(2.10)

for some error terms
\Psi,\Sigma,B,\Theta,\eta_{\gamma}
where
\Phi_{1},\Pi_{1},A_{2},\delta_{2}
are all explicit periodic expressions in time and will be chosen later together with
\gamma
.

The aim of this paper is to provide a better understanding of the asymptotic behaviour of the Fourier coefficients that play a crusial role to the work of Maliborski-Rostworowski
[
30
]
and the conjectured existence of time-periodic solutions to the Eistein-Klein-Gordon equation in spherical symmetry. Is it also motivated by the work of Hunik-Kostyra-Rostworowski
[
28
]
who established interesting recurrence relations for the interaction coefficients for the
5-
dimensional Einstein equations in vacuum with negative cosmological constant within cohomogenity-two biaxial Bianchi IX ansatz. To place our results in the context of the physics literature, we refer the reader to
[
2
,
9
,
10
,
17
,
25
]
. In our main result, we track down the various cancellations occurring in the potentially resonant terms and establish robust bounds for the Fourier coefficients in the two settings described above.

Theorem 2.1

(Rough version)
.

We establish robust bounds for the interaction coefficients of the various Fourier components in the perturbative expansions around the AdS:

•

Perturbation 1: the series (
2.5
)-(
2.7
)

•

Perturbation 2: the finite sum (
2.8
)-(
2.10
).

For a precise version, see Propositions
3.1
and
4.1
-
4.6
respectively.

2.2.
Acknowledgments

The author would like to thank Professor Jacques Smulevici for very useful comments and insights and gratefully acknowledges the support of the ERC grant 714408 GEOWAKI under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation program. The author also acknowledges funding by the Alexander von Humboldt Foundation endowed by the Federal Ministry of Education and Research, by the ERC Consolidator Grant 772249 as well as by the Germany’s Excellence Strategy EXC 2044 390685587, Mathematics Munster: Dynamics-Geometry-Structure.

2.3.
Preliminaries

As we will see, there are subspaces from which these Fourier constants decay and subspaces from which they grow. On the one hand, in order to establish the decay estimates, we will use

•

the leading order terms of the eigenfunctions and their weighted derivatives (Lemma
2.2
and Remark
2.3
)

•

the asymptotic behaviour of specific oscillatory integrals (Lemma
2.6
)

•

integration by parts (Lemma
2.8
and Lemma
2.9
)

The second is based on

•

Dirichlet-Kernel-type identities (Lemma
2.5
)

•

carefully chosen anti-derivatives

On the other hand, for the growth estimates, we will only use

•

Holder’s inequality

•

L^{\infty}-
bounds for quantities related to the eigenfunctions (Lemma
2.4
)

To begin with, we prove the first auxiliary result.

Lemma 2.2

(Closed formulas)
.

For all
x\in[0,\pi/2]
and
i=0,1,\dots
, we have

\displaystyle e_{i}(x)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}\left(\omega_{i}\frac{\sin\left(\omega_{i}x\right)}{\tan(x)}-\cos\left(\omega_{i}x\right)\right),

\displaystyle\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{i}}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}\left(\omega_{i}\frac{\cos\left(\omega_{i}x\right)}{\tan(x)}-\frac{\sin\left(\omega_{i}x\right)}{\tan^{2}(x)}\right),

Furthermore, both
\{e_{i}:i=0,1,2,\dots\}
and
\{e_{i}^{\prime}/\omega_{i}:i=0,1,2,\dots\}
form an orthogonal basis for
L^{2}\left(\left[0,\pi/2\right]\right)
with respect to the weighted inner product (
1.4
), namely

\displaystyle(e_{i}|e_{j})=\delta_{i,j},\quad(e_{i}^{\prime}|e_{j}^{\prime})=\omega_{i}^{2}\delta_{i,j}

and, for all
i,j=0,1,\dots,
. Here,
\delta_{i,j}
stands for the Kronecker’s delta.

Proof.

For the first part, we make use of the facts

\displaystyle P^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}}_{j}(z)=\frac{2\Gamma(j+2)}{\Gamma(j+3)}\frac{d}{dz}P^{-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}}_{j+1}(z),\quad P^{-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}}_{j+1}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\Gamma(j+\frac{3}{2})}{\Gamma(j+2)}\frac{\cos(\omega_{j}x)}{\cos(x)}

where
x=x(z)=\frac{1}{2}\arccos(z)
and
j=0,1,\dots
. These identities can be found in Chapter 4, page 60, equation (4.1.8) and Chapter 4, page 63, equation (4.21.7) in Szegö’s book
[
36
]
. Then, the closed formula for
e_{j}
follows by the chain rule. Indeed, we define
z=\cos(2x)
and compute

\displaystyle P^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}}_{j}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Gamma(j+2)}{\Gamma(j+3)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\Gamma(j+\frac{3}{2})}{\Gamma(j+2)}\frac{d}{dz}\left(\frac{\cos(\omega_{j}x)}{\cos(x)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\Gamma(j+\frac{3}{2})}{\Gamma(j+3)}\frac{dx}{dz}\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{\cos(\omega_{j}x)}{\cos(x)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\Gamma(j+\frac{3}{2})}{\Gamma(j+3)}\frac{-1}{2\sin(2x)}\frac{-\omega_{j}\cos(x)\sin(\omega_{j}x)+\cos(\omega_{j}x)\sin(x)}{\cos^{2}(x)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\Gamma(j+\frac{3}{2})}{\Gamma(j+3)}\left(\frac{\omega_{j}}{2\sin(x)\cos^{2}(x)}\sin(\omega_{j}x)-\frac{1}{2\cos^{3}(x)}\cos(\omega_{j}x)\right)

and so

\displaystyle e_{j}(x)

\displaystyle:=2\frac{\sqrt{j!(j+2)!}}{\Gamma(j+\frac{3}{2})}\cos^{3}(x)P^{\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}}_{j}(\cos(2x))

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{j!(j+2)!}}{\Gamma(j+\frac{3}{2})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\Gamma(j+\frac{3}{2})}{\Gamma(j+3)}\left(\omega_{j}\frac{\cos(x)}{\sin(x)}\sin(\omega_{j}x)-\cos(\omega_{j}x)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\sqrt{j!(j+2)!}}{\Gamma(j+3)}\left(\omega_{j}\frac{\sin(\omega_{j}x)}{\tan(x)}-\cos(\omega_{j}x)\right).

Finally, since
j
is an integer, we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{j!(j+2)!}}{\Gamma(j+3)}=\frac{\sqrt{j!(j+2)!}}{(j+2)!}=\sqrt{\frac{j!}{(j+2)!}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{(j+1)(j+2)}}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}

The closed formula for
e_{j}^{\prime}
follows by differentiating the closed formula for
e_{j}
.
Using these formulas, the orthogonality properties are straightforward. For the fact that the set
\{e_{j}:j=0,1,2,\dots\}
forms a basis for
L^{2}\left(\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]\right)
with respect to the weighted inner product (
1.4
), see the Appendix in the work of Bachelot
[
1
]
. In order to show that
\{\frac{e^{\prime}_{j}}{\omega_{j}}:j=0,1,2,\dots\}
also forms a basis for the same function space, one has to prove that

\displaystyle\left(f\Big|\frac{e^{\prime}_{j}}{\omega_{j}}\right)=0,\quad\forall j=0,1,2,\dots\Longrightarrow f=0.

To this end, we define

\displaystyle F(x):=\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}f(y)dy

and use the fact that

\displaystyle-(\tan^{2}(x)e_{j}^{\prime}(x))^{\prime}=\omega_{j}^{2}\tan^{2}(x)e_{j}(x)

which follows from
L[e_{j}]=\omega_{j}^{2}e_{j}
together with integration by parts to compute

\displaystyle 0=\left(f\Big|\frac{e^{\prime}_{j}}{\omega_{j}}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}f(x)\frac{e^{\prime}_{j}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\tan^{2}(x)dx=-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F^{\prime}(x)\frac{e^{\prime}_{j}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\tan^{2}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F(x)\left(\frac{e^{\prime}_{j}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\tan^{2}(x)\right)^{\prime}dx=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F(x)\left(\frac{e^{\prime}_{j}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\tan^{2}(x)\right)^{\prime}dx

\displaystyle=-\omega_{j}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F(x)e_{j}(x)dx

for all
j=0,1,2,\dots
. Now, we use the fact that
\{e_{j}:j=0,1,2,\dots\}
forms a basis to get
F=0
which in turn implies
f=0
.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

We find the leading order terms

\displaystyle e_{i}(x)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\left(\frac{\omega_{i}}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}\frac{\sin\left(\omega_{i}x\right)}{\tan(x)}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}\cos\left(\omega_{i}x\right)\right)\simeq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\sin\left(\omega_{i}x\right)}{\tan(x)},

\displaystyle\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{i}}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\left(\frac{\omega_{i}}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}\frac{\cos\left(\omega_{i}x\right)}{\tan(x)}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}\frac{\sin\left(\omega_{i}x\right)}{\tan^{2}(x)}\right)\simeq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\cos\left(\omega_{i}x\right)}{\tan(x)},

as
i\longrightarrow\infty
and for all
x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
. These estimates are uniform with respect to the weighted
L^{2}-
norm

\displaystyle\|f\|:=\left(\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}f^{2}(x)\tan^{2}(x)dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Indeed, for large
i
, we estimate

\displaystyle\left\|e_{i}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\sin\left(\omega_{i}\cdot\right)}{\tan}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\left(\frac{\omega_{i}}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}-1\right)\left\|\frac{\sin(\omega_{i}\cdot)}{\tan}\right\|+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}\left\|\cos(\omega_{i}\cdot)\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\omega_{i}}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}-1+\sqrt{\frac{\omega_{i}}{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}}},

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}}{\omega_{i}}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\cos\left(\omega_{i}\cdot\right)}{\tan}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\left(\frac{\omega_{i}}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}-1\right)\left\|\frac{\cos(\omega_{i}\cdot)}{\tan}\right\|+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}\left\|\frac{\sin(\omega_{i}\cdot)}{\tan^{2}}\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\omega_{i}}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}-1+\sqrt{\frac{\omega_{i}}{\omega_{i}^{2}-1}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{i}}},

since

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\sin(\omega_{i}\cdot)}{\tan}\right\|^{2}=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\sin^{2}(\omega_{i}x)dx=\frac{\pi}{4},\quad\left\|\frac{\cos(\omega_{i}\cdot)}{\tan}\right\|^{2}=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(\omega_{i}x)dx=\frac{\pi}{4},

\displaystyle\left\|\cos(\omega_{i}\cdot)\right\|^{2}=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(\omega_{i}x)\tan^{2}(x)dx\lesssim\omega_{i},\quad\left\|\frac{\sin(\omega_{i}\cdot)}{\tan^{2}}\right\|^{2}=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin^{2}(\omega_{i}x)}{\tan^{2}(x)}dx\lesssim\omega_{i}.

(2.11)

The proof of (
2.11
) is given in the Appendix, see Lemma
A.1
.

Next, we prove
L^{\infty}-
bounds for quantities related to the eigenfunctions.

Lemma 2.4

(
L^{\infty}
bounds)
.

For all
j=0,1,2,\dots
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left|e_{j}(x)\right|\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}~\omega_{j},\quad\sup_{x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left|\tan(x)\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}},

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left|\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{j}(y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\omega_{j}}.

Proof.

For the first estimate, we define the oscillating part of
e_{j}(x)
, namely

\displaystyle f_{j}(x):=\omega_{j}\frac{\sin(\omega_{j}x)}{\tan(x)}-\cos(\omega_{j}x),

for all
x\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
and
j=0,1,2,\dots
and compute its derivative

\displaystyle f_{j}^{\prime}(x)=\frac{\omega_{j}}{\tan(x)}\left(\omega_{j}\cos(\omega_{j}x)-\frac{\sin(\omega_{j}x)}{\tan(x)}\right)=\omega_{j}\frac{\cos(\omega_{j}x)}{\tan(x)}\left(\omega_{j}-\frac{\tan(\omega_{j}x)}{\tan(x)}\right).

Hence, for all
x\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
j=0,1,2,\dots
, the equation

\displaystyle f_{j}^{\prime}(x)=0\Longleftrightarrow\tan(\omega_{j}x)=\omega_{j}\tan(x)

has countable many solutions, say
x=x_{j}^{\star}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
. Then,

\displaystyle f_{j}(x_{j}^{\star})

\displaystyle=\cos(\omega_{j}x_{j}^{\star})\Bigg(\omega_{j}\frac{\tan(\omega_{j}x_{j}^{\star})}{\tan(x_{j}^{\star})}-1\Bigg)=\cos(\omega_{j}x_{j}^{\star})\Bigg(\omega_{j}\frac{\omega_{j}\tan(x_{j}^{\star})}{\tan(x_{j}^{\star})}-1\Bigg)=\cos(\omega_{j}x_{j}^{\star})\big(\omega_{j}^{2}-1\big).

Now, since

\displaystyle f_{j}(0)=\lim_{x\rightarrow 0}f_{j}(x)=\omega_{j}^{2}-1,\quad\quad f_{j}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\lim_{x\rightarrow\frac{\pi}{2}}f_{j}(x)=0,

we get

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left|f_{j}(x)\right|=\max\left\{\left|f_{j}(0)\right|,\left|f_{j}(x_{j}^{\star})\right|,\left|f_{j}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\right|\right\}\leq\omega_{j}^{2}-1

and finally

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left|e_{j}(x)\right|=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}\sup_{x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left|f_{j}(x)\right|=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\omega_{j}.

For the second estimate, we observe that

\displaystyle\tan(x)\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}\left(\omega_{j}\cos(\omega_{j}x)-\frac{\sin(\omega_{j}x)}{\tan(x)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}\left(\omega_{j}\cos(\omega_{j}x)-\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\omega_{j}\frac{\sin(\omega_{j}x)}{\tan(x)}+\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\cos(\omega_{j}x)-\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\cos(\omega_{j}x)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}\left(\omega_{j}\cos(\omega_{j}x)-\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\left(\omega_{j}\frac{\sin(\omega_{j}x)}{\tan(x)}-\cos(\omega_{j}x)\right)-\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\cos(\omega_{j}x)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}\left(\left(\omega_{j}-\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\right)\cos(\omega_{j}x)-\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}f_{j}(x)\right).

Hence, by triangular inequality,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left|\tan(x)\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}\left(\left(\omega_{j}-\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\right)+\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\sup_{x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left|f_{j}(x)\right|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}\left(\left(\omega_{j}-\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\right)+\frac{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}{\omega_{j}}\right)\leq\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}},

for all
j=0,1,2,\dots
. For the last estimate, we define

\displaystyle g_{j}(x):=\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{j}(y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy

for all
x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
and
j=0,1,2,\dots
and compute its derivative

\displaystyle g_{j}^{\prime}(x)=-e_{j}(x)\sin(x)\cos(x).

As above, for all
x\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
j=0,1,2,\dots
, the equation

\displaystyle g_{j}^{\prime}(x)=0\Longleftrightarrow\omega_{j}\sin(\omega_{j}x)=\cos(\omega_{j}x)\tan(x)

has countable many solutions, say
x=x_{j}^{\prime}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
. Then,

\displaystyle g_{j}(x_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{x_{j}^{\prime}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{j}(y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}\left(\omega_{j}\int_{x_{j}^{\prime}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\sin(\omega_{j}y)\cos^{2}(y)dy-\int_{x_{j}^{\prime}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos(\omega_{j}y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}\frac{\cos(\omega_{j}x_{j}^{\prime})}{\omega_{j}^{2}-4}\Big(-1-2\sin^{2}(x_{j}^{\prime})+\omega_{j}^{2}\cos^{2}(x_{j}^{\prime})+3\sin^{2}(x_{j}^{\prime})\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}\frac{\cos(\omega_{j}x_{j}^{\prime})}{\omega_{j}^{2}-4}(\omega_{j}^{2}-1)\cos^{2}(x_{j}^{\prime})=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}{\omega_{j}^{2}-4}\cos(\omega_{j}x_{j}^{\prime})\cos^{2}(x_{j}^{\prime})

and since

\displaystyle g_{j}(0)=\lim_{x\rightarrow 0}g_{j}(x)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}{\omega_{j}^{2}-4},\quad\quad g_{j}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)=\lim_{x\rightarrow\frac{\pi}{2}}g_{j}(x)=0,

we get

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left|g_{j}(x)\right|\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\sqrt{\omega_{j}^{2}-1}}{\omega_{j}^{2}-4}\leq\frac{2}{\omega_{j}},

valid for all
j=0,1,2,\dots
, which concludes the proof.
∎

For future reference, we also prove the following Dirichlet-Kernel-type identities.

Lemma 2.5

(Dirichlet-Kernel-type identities)
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\sin((2n+1)x)}{\sin(x)}=1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}\cos(2\mu x),

\displaystyle\frac{\cos((2n+1)x)}{\cos(x)}=(-1)^{n}\left(1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}(-1)^{\mu}\cos(2\mu x)\right),

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(2nx)}{\tan(x)}=1+\cos(2nx)+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{n-1}\cos(2\mu x).

Proof.

The first result is well-known (Dirichlet Kernel). For the second, we use the first one and just replace
x
by
x+\pi/2
,

\displaystyle 1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}(-1)^{\mu}\cos(2\mu x)=1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}\cos(2\mu x+\pi\mu)=1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}\cos\left(2\mu\left(x+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin\left((2n+1)\left(x+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\right)}{\sin\left(x+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)}=\frac{\sin((2n+1)x)\cos\left((2n+1)\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+\cos((2n+1)x)\sin\left((2n+1)\frac{\pi}{2}\right)}{\cos(x)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\frac{\cos((2n+1)x)}{\cos(x)}.

For the third, we observe that

\displaystyle\frac{\sin(2nx)}{\tan(x)}+\cos(2nx)=\frac{\sin(2nx)\cos(x)+\cos(2nx)\sin(x)}{\sin(x)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(2n+1)x}{\sin(x)}=1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}\cos(2\mu x)=1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{n-1}\cos(2\mu x)+2\cos(2nx),

from which the result follows.
∎

Finally, we establish the asymptotic behaviour of specific oscillatory integrals which will appear later.

Lemma 2.6

(Oscillatory integrals)
.

For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin((2a+1)x)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin(2ax)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{N}}\right),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{3}(x)\frac{\sin((2a+1)x)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{N}}\right),

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\pi}{a}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\cos(2ax)}{\tan^{2}(x)}dx

\displaystyle=ca+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{3}}\right),

as
a\longrightarrow\infty
with
a\in\mathbb{N}
. Here,

\displaystyle c:=\frac{1}{\pi}-\pi+2\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sin(2\pi y)}{y}dy\simeq 0.01302.

Proof.

For the first integral, we use Lemma
2.5
to infer

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin((2a+1)x)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos(x)\frac{\sin((2a+1)x)}{\sin(x)}dx=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos(x)\left(1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{a}\cos(2\mu x)\right)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos(x)dx+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{a}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos(x)\cos(2\mu x)dx

\displaystyle=1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{a}\frac{(-1)^{\mu}}{1-4\mu^{2}}=1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\mu}}{1-4\mu^{2}}-2\sum_{\mu=a+1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\mu}}{1-4\mu^{2}}

\displaystyle=1+2\frac{\pi-2}{4}-2\sum_{\mu=a+1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\mu}}{1-4\mu^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{2}+2\sum_{\mu=a+1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\mu}}{4\mu^{2}-1}=\frac{\pi}{2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{2}}\right).

The second integral follows similarly. Lemma
2.5
implies

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin(2ax)}{\tan(x)}dx=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\left(1+\cos(2ax)+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{a-1}\cos(2\mu x)\right)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)dx+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\cos(2ax)dx+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{a-1}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\cos(2\mu x)dx

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{4}+0+2\frac{\pi}{8}=\frac{\pi}{2},

for all
a\geq 3
, since

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)dx=\frac{\pi}{4},\quad\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\cos(2\lambda x)dx=\begin{cases}\frac{\pi}{8},&\lambda=1\\
0,&\lambda\geq 2.\end{cases}

Next, for the third integral, Lemma
2.5
also yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{3}(x)\frac{\sin((2a+1)x)}{\tan(x)}dx=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{4}(x)\frac{\sin((2a+1)x)}{\sin(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{4}(x)\Bigg(1+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{a}\cos(2\mu x)\Bigg)dx=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{4}(x)dx+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{a}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{4}(x)\cos(2\mu x)dx

\displaystyle=\frac{3\pi}{16}+2\frac{\pi}{8}+2\frac{\pi}{32}=\frac{\pi}{2},

for all
a\geq 3
, since

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{4}(x)dx=\frac{3\pi}{16},\quad\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\cos(2\lambda x)dx=\begin{cases}\frac{\pi}{8},&\lambda=1\\
\frac{\pi}{32},&\lambda=2\\
0,&\lambda\geq 3.\end{cases}

Finally, we conclude with the fourth integral. First, observe that

\displaystyle 2\frac{\cos(2ax)}{\tan^{2}(x)}+4a\frac{\sin(2ax)}{\tan(x)}+\Bigg(2\frac{\cos(2ax)}{\tan(x)}+\frac{\sin(2ax)}{a}\Bigg)^{\prime}=0

and therefore, for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{\epsilon}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}2\frac{\cos(2ax)}{\tan^{2}(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\epsilon}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\left(-\Bigg(2\frac{\cos(2ax)}{\tan(x)}+\frac{\sin(2ax)}{a}\Bigg)^{\prime}-4a\frac{\sin(2ax)}{\tan(x)}\right)dx

\displaystyle=2\frac{\cos(2a\epsilon)}{\tan(\epsilon)}+\frac{\sin(2a\epsilon)}{a}-4a\int_{\epsilon}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin(2ax)}{\tan(x)}dx.

Second, we set
\epsilon=\frac{\pi}{a}
and get
\cos(2a\epsilon)=1,\sin(2a\epsilon)=0
. Now, Lemma
2.5
shows that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin(2ax)}{\tan(x)}dx=\frac{\pi}{2}+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos(2ax)dx+2\sum_{\mu=1}^{a-1}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos(2\mu x)dx=\frac{\pi}{2}+0+0=\frac{\pi}{2},

valid for all
a\geq 1
, and we change variables
y=ax/\pi
to infer

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\pi}{a}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\cos(2ax)}{\tan^{2}(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tan(\frac{\pi}{a})}-2a\int_{\frac{\pi}{a}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin(2ax)}{\tan(x)}dx=\frac{1}{\tan(\frac{\pi}{a})}-2a\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{a}}\frac{\sin(2ax)}{\tan(x)}dx\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tan(\frac{\pi}{a})}-a\pi+2\pi\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sin(2\pi y)}{\tan(\frac{\pi y}{a})}dy,

for all
a\geq 2
. Now, for all
y\in[0,1]
and
a\longrightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tan(\frac{\pi y}{a})}=\frac{a}{\pi}\frac{1}{y}-\frac{\pi}{3a}y+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{y}{a}\right)^{3}\right)

and hence

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sin(2\pi y)}{\tan(\frac{\pi y}{a})}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sin(2\pi y)}{y}dy-\frac{\pi}{3a}\int_{0}^{1}y\sin(2\pi y)dy+\int_{0}^{1}\sin(2\pi y)\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{y}{a}\right)^{3}\right)dy

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sin(2\pi y)}{y}dy-\frac{\pi}{3a}\frac{-1}{2\pi}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sin(2\pi y)}{y}dy+\frac{1}{6a}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{3}}\right)

from which we conclude

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{\pi}{a}}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\cos(2ax)}{\tan^{2}(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tan(\frac{\pi}{a})}-a\pi+2\pi\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sin(2\pi y)}{\tan(\frac{\pi y}{a})}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tan(\frac{\pi}{a})}-a\pi+2a\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sin(2\pi y)}{y}dy+\frac{\pi}{3a}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\pi}-\frac{\pi}{3a}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{3}}\right)-a\pi+2a\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sin(2\pi y)}{y}dy+\frac{\pi}{3a}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{3}}\right)

\displaystyle=a\left(\frac{1}{\pi}-\pi+2\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sin(2\pi y)}{y}dy\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{3}}\right),

that completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

A straightforward computation yields

\displaystyle e_{0}(x)=4\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\cos^{3}(x)

and hence the third integral of Lemma
2.6
also gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{0}(x)\frac{\sin((2a+1)x)}{\tan(x)}dx=2\sqrt{2\pi}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{a^{N}}\right),

for
a\longrightarrow\infty
.

Finally, we will also use standard integration by parts.

Lemma 2.8

(Integration by parts)
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{N}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that
F
is differentiable in
\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)
and continuous in
\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
. Then,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F(x)\cos(ax)dx=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{a^{2k+1}}\left(F^{(2k)}(x)\sin(ax)\right)\Big|_{x=0}^{x=\frac{\pi}{2}}+\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{a^{2k+2}}\left(F^{(2k+1)}(x)\cos(ax)\right)\Big|_{x=0}^{x=\frac{\pi}{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{a^{2N+2}}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F^{(2N+2)}(x)\cos(ax)dx,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F(x)\sin(ax)dx=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{a^{2k+1}}\left(F^{(2k)}(x)\cos(ax)\right)\Big|_{x=0}^{x=\frac{\pi}{2}}+\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{a^{2k+2}}\left(F^{(2k+1)}(x)\sin(ax)\right)\Big|_{x=0}^{x=\frac{\pi}{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{a^{2N+2}}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F^{(2N+2)}(x)\sin(ax)dx.

Proof.

The proof is a straight forward application of integration by parts.
∎

The following result is a direct consequence of Lemma
2.8
.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
b\in\mathbb{N}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that
F
is differentiable in
\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right)
, continuous in
\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
with uniformly bounded derivatives in
\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
. Then, as
b\longrightarrow\infty
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F(x)\cos(2bx)dx=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(2b)^{2k+2}}\left((-1)^{b}F^{(2k+1)}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)-F^{(2k+1)}\left(0\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{b^{2N+2}}\right),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F(x)\sin(2bx)dx=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{(2b)^{2k+1}}\left((-1)^{b}F^{(2k)}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)-F^{(2k)}\left(0\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{b^{2N+2}}\right),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F(x)\cos((2b+1)x)dx=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k+b}}{(2b+1)^{2k+1}}F^{(2k)}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k+1}}{(2b+1)^{2k+2}}F^{(2k+1)}\left(0\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{b^{2N+2}}\right),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F(x)\sin((2b+1)x)dx=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(2b+1)^{2k+1}}F^{(2k)}\left(0\right)+\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k+b}}{(2b+1)^{2k+2}}F^{(2k+1)}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{b^{2N+2}}\right).

Proof.

All these asymptotic expansions follow from Lemma
2.8
just by computing the boundary terms. Note that if
a
is even, namely
a=2b
for some
b\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\sin(a\frac{\pi}{2})=0
and
\cos(a\frac{\pi}{2})=(-1)^{b}
whereas if
a
is odd, namely
a=2b+1
for some
b\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\sin(a\frac{\pi}{2})=(-1)^{b}
and
\cos(a\frac{\pi}{2})=0
. For example, Lemma
2.8
yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F(x)\cos(2bx)dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{a^{2k+2}}\left((-1)^{b}F^{(2k+1)}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)-F^{(2k+1)}\left(0\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{(-1)^{N-1}}{a^{2N+2}}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F^{(2N+2)}(x)\cos(ax)dx.

The other asymptotic expansions follow similarly.
∎

With these auxiliary results at hand we proceed to the main analysis of the Fourier constants.

3.
Perturbations 1: Series

As mentioned above, Maliborski-Rostworowski
[
30
]
considered (
2.1
)-(
2.4
) and provided strong numerical evidence indicating that time-periodic solutions exist for non-generic initial data. To explain their approach and see how the Fourier constants appear, first assume that
(\Phi,\Pi,A,\delta)
are all close to
(0,0,1,0)
and expand
(\Phi,\Pi,A,\delta)
in terms of powers of
\epsilon
using the series (
2.5
)-(
2.7
).

3.1.
Definition of the Fourier constants

First, we compute the density

\displaystyle\Phi^{2}(t,x)+\Pi^{2}(t,x)=\sum_{\lambda=1}^{\infty}r_{2\lambda}\epsilon^{2\lambda}

where, for all
\lambda=0,1,2,\dots
,

\displaystyle r_{2(\lambda+1)}(\tau,x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu=0\\
\mu+\nu=\lambda\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\left(\psi_{2\mu+1}(\tau,x)\psi_{2\nu+1}(\tau,x)+\sigma_{2\mu+1}(\tau,x)\sigma_{2\nu+1}(\tau,x)\right).

Next, we substitute these expressions into (
2.1
)-(
2.4
), collect terms of the same order in
\epsilon
and obtain a hierarchy of equations

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma}\partial_{\tau}\psi_{2\lambda+1}(\tau,x)-\partial_{x}\sigma_{2\lambda+1}(\tau,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu=0\\
\mu+\nu=\lambda\\
(\mu,\nu)\neq(\lambda,0)\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\Big(-\omega_{\gamma,2\nu}\partial_{\tau}\psi_{2\mu+1}(\tau,x)+\partial_{x}\left(\xi_{2\nu}(\tau,x)\sigma_{2\mu+1}(\tau,x)\right)\Big),

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma}\partial_{\tau}\sigma_{2\lambda+1}(\tau,x)+\hbox{\hskip \displaystyle=-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu=0\\

\displaystyle\xi_{2(\lambda+1)}(\tau,x)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{2(\lambda+1)}(\tau,x)-\frac{\cos^{3}(x)}{\sin(x)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu=0\\
\mu+\nu=\lambda\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\int_{0}^{x}r_{2(\mu+1)}(\tau,y)\zeta_{2\nu}(\tau,y)\tan^{2}(y)dy,

\displaystyle\zeta_{2(\lambda+1)}(\tau,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu=0\\
\mu+\nu=\lambda\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\int_{0}^{x}r_{2(\mu+1)}(\tau,y)\zeta_{2\nu}(\tau,y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy,

for all
\lambda=0,1,2,\dots
, together with

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma,0}=\omega_{\gamma},\quad\xi_{0}(\tau,x)=1,\quad\zeta_{0}(\tau,x)=1,\quad\begin{cases}\omega_{\gamma}\partial_{\tau}\psi_{1}(\tau,x)-\partial_{x}\sigma_{1}(\tau,x)=0,\\
\omega_{\gamma}\partial_{\tau}\sigma_{1}(\tau,x)+\hbox{\hskip 411.22394pt\hskip-3.40279pt\hbox{$L$}\hskip-3.40279pt\hskip-411.22394pt\raisebox{1.77776pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-2.59816pt}{\resizebox{48365776}{564336}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-165.94817pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.1pt\mathchar 866\kern 0.1pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-411.22394pt\hskip 411.22394pt}\left[\psi_{1}(\tau,x)\right]=0.\end{cases}

From the set of all eigenvalues
\{e_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
to the linear operator, we choose a dominant mode
e_{\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}
and pick

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\psi_{1}(\tau,x)=\cos(\tau)e^{\prime}_{\gamma}(x),\\
\sigma_{1}(\tau,x)=-\omega_{\gamma}\sin(\tau)e_{\gamma}(x).\end{cases}

Now, for each
\lambda=0,1,2,\dots
, we expand the coefficients
\psi_{2\lambda+1},\sigma_{2\lambda+1},\xi_{2\lambda},\zeta_{2\lambda}
in terms of the eigenvalues of the linearized operator, namely

\displaystyle\psi_{2\lambda+1}(\tau,x)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}f_{2\lambda+1}^{(i)}(\tau)\frac{e^{\prime}_{i}(x)}{\omega_{i}},\quad\sigma_{2\lambda+1}(\tau,x)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}g_{2\lambda+1}^{(i)}(\tau)e_{i}(x),

\displaystyle\xi_{2\lambda}(\tau,x)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}p_{2\lambda}^{(i)}(\tau)e_{i}(x),\quad\zeta_{2\lambda}(\tau,x)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}q_{2\lambda}^{(i)}(\tau)e_{i}(x),

substitute these expressions into the recurrence relations above, take the inner product
(\cdot|e^{\prime}_{m})
for the first equation, the
(\cdot|e_{m})
for all the other equations and use their orthogonality properties
(e^{\prime}_{n}|e^{\prime}_{m})=\omega_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}
,
(e_{n},e_{m})=\delta_{nm}
. Using the notation

\displaystyle\dot{f}(\tau)=\frac{df(\tau)}{d\tau},\quad\ddot{f}(\tau)=\frac{d^{2}f(\tau)}{d\tau^{2}},

we find

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma}\dot{f}_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)=\omega_{m}g_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu=0\\
\mu+\nu=\lambda\\
(\mu,\nu)\neq(\lambda,0)\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\left(-\omega_{\gamma,2\nu}\dot{f}_{2\mu+1}^{(m)}(\tau)+\omega_{m}\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}C_{ij}^{(m)}p_{2\nu}^{(i)}(\tau)g_{2\mu+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\right),

\displaystyle\omega_{\gamma}\dot{g}_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)=-\omega_{m}f_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu=0\\
\mu+\nu=\lambda\\
(\mu,\nu)\neq(\lambda,0)\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\left(\omega_{\gamma,2\nu}\dot{g}_{2\mu+1}^{(m)}(\tau)+\omega_{m}\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\overline{C}_{ij}^{(m)}p_{2\nu}^{(i)}(\tau)f_{2\mu+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\right),

\displaystyle p_{2(\lambda+1)}^{(m)}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho,k,\nu=0\\
\rho+k+\nu=\lambda\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\sum_{i,j,l=0}^{\infty}\Bigg(\widetilde{A}_{ijl}^{(m)}f_{2\rho+1}^{(i)}(\tau)f_{2k+1}^{(j)}(\tau)+\widetilde{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}g_{2\rho+1}^{(i)}(\tau)g_{2k+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\Bigg)q_{2\nu}^{(l)}(\tau),

\displaystyle q_{2(\lambda+1)}^{(m)}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho,k,\nu=0\\
\rho+k+\nu=\lambda\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\sum_{i,j,l=0}^{\infty}\Bigg(\frac{\overline{A}_{ijl}^{(m)}}{\omega_{m}}f_{2\rho+1}^{(i)}(\tau)f_{2k+1}^{(j)}(\tau)+\frac{\overline{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}}{\omega_{m}}g_{2\rho+1}^{(i)}(\tau)g_{2k+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\Bigg)q_{2\nu}^{(l)}(\tau),

where all the interactions with respect to the spatial variable
x\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
are included in the following Fourier constants

\displaystyle C_{ij}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)e_{j}(x)e_{m}(x)\tan^{2}(x)dx,

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{ij}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\tan^{2}(x)dx,

\displaystyle\overline{A}_{ijl}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{i}}\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}e_{l}(x)\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\frac{\sin^{3}(x)}{\cos(x)}dx,

\displaystyle\overline{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)e_{j}(x)e_{l}(x)\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\frac{\sin^{3}(x)}{\cos(x)}dx,

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}_{ijl}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\overline{A}_{ijl}^{(m)}}{\omega_{m}}-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{i}}\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}e_{l}(x)\left(\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{m}(y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy\right)\tan^{2}(x)dx,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\overline{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}}{\omega_{m}}-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)e_{j}(x)e_{l}(x)\left(\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{m}(y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy\right)\tan^{2}(x)dx.

We also find

\displaystyle f_{1}^{(m)}(\tau)=\omega_{\gamma}\cos(\tau)\delta_{\gamma}^{m},\quad g_{1}^{(m)}(\tau)=-\omega_{\gamma}\sin(\tau)\delta_{\gamma}^{m},\quad p_{0}^{(m)}(\tau)=q_{0}^{(m)}(\tau)=(1|e_{m})

and use Lemma
2.2
to compute

\displaystyle(1|e_{m}):=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{m}(x)\tan^{2}(x)dx=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{\omega_{m}}\sqrt{\omega_{m}^{2}-1},

for all
m=0,1,2,\dots
. In addition, we differentiate the first equation with respect to
\tau
and use the second to obtain the harmonic oscillator equation

\displaystyle\ddot{f}_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)+\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\right)^{2}f_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)=S_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)

(3.1)

where the source term is given by

\displaystyle S_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu=0\\
\mu+\nu=\lambda\\
(\mu,\nu)\neq(\lambda,0)\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\Bigg[-\frac{\omega_{\gamma,2\nu}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\left(\dot{g}_{2\mu+1}^{(m)}(\tau)+\frac{\omega_{\gamma}}{\omega_{m}}\ddot{f}_{2\mu+1}^{(m)}(\tau)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\left(C_{ij}^{(m)}\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(p_{2\nu}^{(i)}(\tau)g_{2\mu+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\right)-\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\overline{C}_{ij}^{(m)}p_{2\nu}^{(i)}(\tau)f_{2\mu+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\right)\Bigg].

Finally, we make use of the variation constants formula to solve (
3.1
) and find

\displaystyle f_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)

\displaystyle=f_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(0)\cos\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\tau\right)+\frac{\omega_{\gamma}}{\omega_{m}}\dot{f}_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(0)\sin\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\tau\right)+\frac{\omega_{\gamma}}{\omega_{m}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\sin\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}(\tau-s)\right)S_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(s)ds.

In conclusion, we get for all
m=0,1,2,\dots
the following recurrence relations. For all
\lambda=1,2,3,\dots
,

\displaystyle f_{1}^{(m)}(\tau)=\omega_{\gamma}\cos(\tau)\delta_{\gamma}^{m},

\displaystyle f_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)=f_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(0)\cos\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\tau\right)+\frac{\omega_{\gamma}}{\omega_{m}}\dot{f}_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(0)\sin\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\tau\right)+\frac{\omega_{\gamma}}{\omega_{m}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\sin\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}(\tau-s)\right)S_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(s)ds,

\displaystyle S_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)=\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu=0\\
\mu+\nu=\lambda\\
(\mu,\nu)\neq(\lambda,0)\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\Bigg[-\frac{\omega_{\gamma,2\nu}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\left(\dot{g}_{2\mu+1}^{(m)}(\tau)+\frac{\omega_{\gamma}}{\omega_{m}}\ddot{f}_{2\mu+1}^{(m)}(\tau)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\left(C_{ij}^{(m)}\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(p_{2\nu}^{(i)}(\tau)g_{2\mu+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\right)-\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\overline{C}_{ij}^{(m)}p_{2\nu}^{(i)}(\tau)f_{2\mu+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle g_{1}^{(m)}(\tau)=-\omega_{\gamma}\sin(\tau)\delta_{\gamma}^{m},

\displaystyle g_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)=\frac{\omega_{\gamma}}{\omega_{m}}\dot{f}_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu,\nu=0\\
\mu+\nu=\lambda\\
(\mu,\nu)\neq(\lambda,0)\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\Bigg[\frac{\omega_{\gamma,2\nu}}{\omega_{m}}\dot{f}_{2\mu+1}^{(m)}(\tau)-\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}C_{ij}^{(m)}p_{2\nu}^{(i)}(\tau)g_{2\mu+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\Bigg],

\displaystyle p_{0}^{(m)}(\tau)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{\omega_{m}}\sqrt{\omega_{m}^{2}-1},

\displaystyle p_{2(\lambda+1)}^{(m)}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho,k,\nu=0\\
\rho+k+\nu=\lambda\\
\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\sum_{i,j,l=0}^{\infty}\Bigg[\widetilde{A}_{ijl}^{(m)}f_{2\rho+1}^{(i)}(\tau)f_{2k+1}^{(j)}(\tau)+\widetilde{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}g_{2\rho+1}^{(i)}(\tau)g_{2k+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\Bigg]q_{2\nu}^{(l)}(\tau),

\displaystyle q_{0}^{(m)}(\tau)=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{\omega_{m}}\sqrt{\omega_{m}^{2}-1},

\displaystyle q_{2(\lambda+1)}^{(m)}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho,k,\nu=0\\
\rho+k+\nu=\lambda\end{subarray}}^{\lambda}\sum_{i,j,l=0}^{\infty}\Bigg[\frac{\overline{A}_{ijl}^{(m)}}{\omega_{m}}f_{2\rho+1}^{(i)}(\tau)f_{2k+1}^{(j)}(\tau)+\frac{\overline{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}}{\omega_{m}}g_{2\rho+1}^{(i)}(\tau)g_{2k+1}^{(j)}(\tau)\Bigg]q_{2\nu}^{(l)}(\tau).

3.2.
time-periodic solutions and secular terms

As pointed out in
[
30
]
, non-periodic terms appear naturally when the source term
S_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)
has terms of the form
\cos(\omega_{m}\tau/\omega_{\gamma})
or
\sin(\omega_{m}\tau/\omega_{\gamma})
in its Fourier expansion. Indeed, we assume that, for some
\lambda=1,2,3,\dots
,

\displaystyle S_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)=\sum_{a\in I_{\lambda}}S_{1,2\lambda+1,a}^{(m)}\cos(ax)+\sum_{b\in J_{\lambda}}S_{2,2\lambda+1,b}^{(m)}\sin(bx),

and in addition there exists an index
m=0,1,2,\dots
such that
\omega_{m}/\omega_{\gamma}:=a\in I_{\lambda}
.
Then, the integral

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\sin\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}(\tau-s)\right)S_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(s)ds

produces a non-periodic term since

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\tau}\sin\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}(\tau-s)\right)\cos\left(as\right)ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\tau}\sin\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}(\tau-s)\right)\cos\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}s\right)ds=\frac{1}{2}\tau\sin\left(\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{\gamma}}\tau\right).

Such secular terms are also produced when there exists an
m=0,1,2,\dots
such that
\omega_{m}/\omega_{\gamma}:=b\in J_{\lambda}
.
In other words,

\displaystyle\forall\lambda=0,1,2,\dots,~\exists\text{~a set~}\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}:~\forall m\in\mathcal{N}_{\lambda},~f_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)\text{~contains non-periodic terms}.

Maliborski and Rostworowski
[
30
]
were able to numerically cancel these secular terms by prescribing the initial data
(f_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(0),\dot{f}_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(0))
. To explain their approach, we take
f_{1}^{(\gamma)}(0)=1
and
f_{2\lambda+1}^{(\gamma)}(0)=0
for
\lambda=1,2,3,\dots
. First, they choose
\dot{f}_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(0)=0
for all
\lambda=0,1,2,\dots
and all
m=0,1,2,\dots
to ensure that the source term
S_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(\tau)
is a series only of cosines. Then, they observed that the fixed index
\gamma
belongs in
\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda=0,1,2,\dots
and there is only one secular term in
f_{2\lambda+1}^{(\gamma)}(\tau)
which can be removed by choosing the frequency shift
\omega_{\gamma,2(\lambda-1)}
. Furthermore, for all
m\in\mathcal{N}_{\lambda}\setminus\{\gamma\}
, there are some secular terms which cancel by the structure of the equations, some secular terms cancel by choosing some initial data but some initial data remain free variables at this stage. They choose these free variables together with
\omega_{\gamma,2\lambda}
to cancel the secular terms in the
f_{2(\lambda+1)+1}(\tau)
. For more details see
[
30
]
. However, there is no proof based on rigorous arguments ensuring that this procedure works for all
\lambda
.

3.3.
Choice of the initial data

For example, we fix
\gamma=0
and choose

\displaystyle\dot{f}_{2\lambda+1}^{(m)}(0)=0,\quad\forall\lambda\geq 0,\quad\forall m\geq 0.

First, we use the recurrence relation above and find periodic expressions for
p_{2}^{(m)}(\tau)
and
q_{2}^{(m)}(\tau)
due to the periodicity of
f_{1}^{(m)}(\tau)
and
g_{1}^{(m)}(\tau)
. Second, we compute

\displaystyle S_{3}^{(m)}(\tau)=A_{3}^{(m)}\cos(\tau)+B_{3}^{(m)}\cos(3\tau),

for some sequences
\{A_{3}^{(m)}\}_{m=0,1,\dots}
and
\{B_{3}^{(m)}\}_{m=0,1,\dots}
. Then, the equation

\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{m}}{\omega_{0}}=\frac{3+2m}{3}=1+\frac{2}{3}m\in\{1,3\}

has two solutions
m\in\mathcal{N}_{3}:=\{0,3\}
. Based on the discussion above, we get two secular terms in the list
\{f_{3}^{(m)}(\tau):m=0,1,2,\dots\}
, one for
m=0
and one for
m=3
. We see that the secular term for
m=0
can be canceled by choosing the frequency shift
\omega_{0,2}
whereas the secular term for
m=3
cancels by the structure of the equations meaning
B_{3}^{(3)}=0
without any choice of the initial data
\{f_{3}^{(m)}(0):m=0,1,2,\dots\}
. Hence, all these are free variables at this stage (i.e. for
\lambda=3
) and will be chosen later to cancel all the secular terms in
f_{5}^{(m)}(\tau)
. Specifically, we get

\displaystyle f_{3}^{(0)}(\tau)=\left(\frac{765}{128\pi}+f_{3}^{(0)}(0)\right)\cos(\tau)-\frac{765}{128\pi}\cos(3\tau)+\left(\omega_{0,2}-\frac{153}{4\pi}\right)\tau\sin(\tau),

\displaystyle f_{3}^{(1)}(\tau)=\frac{6183}{256\pi}\sqrt{3}\cos(\tau)+\frac{765}{256\pi}\sqrt{3}\cos(3\tau)+\left(f_{3}^{(1)}(0)-\frac{1737}{64\pi}\sqrt{3}\right)\cos\left(\frac{5}{3}\tau\right),

\displaystyle f_{3}^{(2)}(\tau)=\frac{3717}{3200\pi}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\cos(\tau)+\frac{441}{128\pi}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\cos(3\tau)+\left(f_{3}^{(2)}(0)-\frac{7371}{1600\pi}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\right)\cos\left(\frac{7}{3}\tau\right),

and so forth. We choose

\displaystyle\omega_{0,2}=\frac{153}{4\pi}

to ensure the periodicity of
f_{3}^{(0)}(\tau)
. Once all secular terms in
f_{3}^{(m)}(\tau)
are removed, the periodic expression for
f_{3}^{(m)}(\tau)
implies a periodic expression also for
g_{3}^{(m)}(\tau)
.

3.4.
Growth and decay of the Fourier constants

In this section, we focus on the asymptotic behaviour of all the Fourier constants which appear using this approach. We shall use the notation

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}f(a\pm b\pm c)=f(a+b+c)+f(a+b-c)+f(a-b+c)+f(a-b-c),

that is summation with respect to all possible combinations of plus and minus. Furthermore, expressions like
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m}
stand not only for
\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{m}
and
\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{m}
but also for
\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{m}
and
\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{m}
, that is considering all possible combinations of plus and minus. Specifically, we prove the following result the proof of which is based on the leading order terms (Remark
2.3
) together with the asymptotic behavior of the oscillatory integrals (Lemma
2.6
), the orthogonality properties (Lemma
2.2
) and the
L^{\infty}-
bounds (Lemma
2.4
).

Proposition 3.1

(Growth and decay estimates for the Fourier constants: Perturbation 1)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
. The following growth and decay estimates hold.

Growth and decay estimates for
C_{ij}^{(m)}
and
\overline{C}_{ij}^{(m)}
as
i,j,m\longrightarrow+\infty

Constant

\exists~~\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m}\longarrownot\longrightarrow\infty

\forall~~\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m}\longrightarrow\infty

\displaystyle C_{ij}^{(m)}

\mathcal{O}\left(\omega_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{ij}^{(m)}

\mathcal{O}\left(\omega_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}+\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m})^{2}}\right)

Growth and decay estimates for
A_{ijl}^{(m)},B_{ijl}^{(m)},\overline{A}_{ijl}^{(m)}
and
\overline{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}
as
i,j,l,m\longrightarrow+\infty

Constant

\exists~~\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\longarrownot\longrightarrow\infty

\forall~~\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\longrightarrow\infty

\displaystyle\overline{A}_{ijl}^{(m)},\overline{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\omega_{l}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m})^{N}}\right)

\displaystyle A_{ijl}^{(m)},B_{ijl}^{(m)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\omega_{l}}{\omega_{m}}\right)

\displaystyle~~~\frac{1}{\omega_{m}}\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\right)^{N}}\right)

Proof.

First, observe that
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m}
are all odd, namely

\displaystyle\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{m}

\displaystyle=2(i-j+m+1)+1,\quad\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{m}=2(i-j-m-2)+1,

\displaystyle\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{m}

\displaystyle=2(i+j+m+4)+1,\quad\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{m}=2(i+j-m+1)+1.

For large values of
i,j,m
and in the case where all
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m}\longrightarrow\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{ij}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)e_{j}(x)e_{m}(x)\tan^{2}(x)dx\simeq\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{3}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin(\omega_{i}x)\sin(\omega_{j}x)\sin(\omega_{m}x)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{3}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin((\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{m})x)}{\tan(x)}dx-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin((\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{m})x)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin((\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{m})x)}{\tan(x)}dx+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin((\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{m})x)}{\tan(x)}dx\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{3}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{2}+\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m})^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m})^{2}}\right).

On the other hand, for large values of
i,j,m
such that some
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m}\longarrownot\longrightarrow\infty
, Holder’s inequality implies

\displaystyle\left|C_{ij}^{(m)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)e_{j}(x)e_{m}(x)\tan^{2}(x)dx\right|\leq\left\|e_{i}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|e_{j}\tan\right\|_{L^{2}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|e_{m}\tan\right\|_{L^{2}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\lesssim\omega_{i}.

Similarly, for large values of
i,j,m
and in the case where all
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m}\longrightarrow\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{C}_{ij}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\tan^{2}(x)dx\simeq\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{3}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin(\omega_{i}x)\cos(\omega_{j}x)\cos(\omega_{m}x)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{3}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin((\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{m})x)}{\tan(x)}dx+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin((\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{m})x)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin((\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{m})x)}{\tan(x)}dx+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sin((\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{m})x)}{\tan(x)}dx\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{3}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{2}+\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m})^{2}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\sqrt{\pi}}+\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m})^{2}}\right).

On the other hand, for large values of
i,j,m
such that some
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{m}\longarrownot\longrightarrow\infty
, Holder’s inequality implies

\displaystyle\left|\overline{C}_{ij}^{(m)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\tan^{2}(x)dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|e_{i}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\tan\right\|_{L^{2}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\tan\right\|_{L^{2}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\lesssim\omega_{i}.

Next, observe that
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}
are all even,

\displaystyle\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}

\displaystyle=2(i-j+l-m),\quad\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}=2(i+j-l-m),

\displaystyle\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}

\displaystyle=2(-3+i-j-l-m),\quad\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}=2(3+i+j+l-m),

\displaystyle\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}

\displaystyle=2(i-j-l+m),\quad\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}=2(6+i+j+l+m),

\displaystyle\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}

\displaystyle=2(3+i-j+l+m),\quad\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}=2(3+i+j-l+m).

Now, for large values of
i,j,l,m
and in the case where all
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\longrightarrow\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\overline{A}_{ijl}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{i}}\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}e_{l}(x)\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\frac{\sin^{3}(x)}{\cos(x)}dx

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{4}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\cos(\omega_{i}x)\cos(\omega_{j}x)\sin(\omega_{l}x)\cos(\omega_{m}x)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{4}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{4}\left(4\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\right)^{N}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\right)^{N}}\right),

whereas, for large values of
i,j,l,m
such that some
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\longarrownot\longrightarrow\infty
, Holder’s inequality implies

\displaystyle\left|\overline{A}_{ijl}^{(m)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{i}}\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}e_{l}(x)\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\frac{\sin^{3}(x)}{\cos(x)}dx\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{i}}\tan(x)\cdot\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\tan(x)\cdot e_{l}(x)\cos^{2}(x)\cdot\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\tan(x)dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}}{\omega_{i}}\tan\right\|_{L^{2}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}}{\omega_{j}}\tan\right\|_{L^{2}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|e_{l}\cos^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}}{\omega_{m}}\tan\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\lesssim\omega_{l}.

Furthermore, for large values of
i,j,l,m
and in the case where all
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\longrightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\overline{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)e_{j}(x)e_{l}(x)\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\frac{\sin^{3}(x)}{\cos(x)}dx

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{4}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin(\omega_{i}x)\sin(\omega_{j}x)\sin(\omega_{l}x)\cos(\omega_{m}x)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{4}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{4}\left(4\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\right)^{N}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\right)^{N}}\right),

whereas, for large values of
i,j,l,m
such that some
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\longarrownot\longrightarrow\infty
, Holder’s inequality implies

\displaystyle\left|\overline{B}_{ijl}^{(m)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)e_{j}(x)e_{l}(x)\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\frac{\sin^{3}(x)}{\cos(x)}dx\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)\tan(x)\cdot e_{j}(x)\tan(x)\cdot e_{l}(x)\cos^{2}(x)\cdot\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{m}}\tan(x)dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|e_{i}\tan\right\|_{L^{2}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|e_{j}\tan\right\|_{L^{2}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|e_{l}\cos^{2}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|\frac{e_{m}^{\prime}}{\omega_{m}}\tan\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\lesssim\omega_{l}.

Next, we use once more Remark
2.3
to compute

\displaystyle\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{m}(y)\cos(y)\sin(y)dy\simeq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\sin(\omega_{m}y)\cos^{2}(y)dy

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\left(\frac{\sin(\omega_{m}x)\sin(2x)}{\omega_{m}^{2}-4}-\frac{2\cos(\omega_{m}x)}{\omega_{m}(\omega_{m}^{2}-4)}+\frac{\omega_{m}\cos^{2}(x)\cos(\omega_{m}x)}{\omega_{m}^{2}-4}\right)\simeq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{1}{\omega_{m}}\cos^{2}(x)\cos(\omega_{m}x),

as
m\longrightarrow\infty
. Hence, for large values of
i,j,l,m
and in the case where all
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\longrightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle A_{ijl}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{i}}\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}e_{l}(x)\left(\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{m}(y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy\right)\tan^{2}(x)dx

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{4}\frac{1}{\omega_{m}}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\cos(\omega_{i}x)\cos(\omega_{j}x)\sin(\omega_{l}x)\cos(\omega_{m}x)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{4}\frac{1}{\omega_{m}}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx+\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}+\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx-\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\cos^{2}(x)\frac{\sin\left(\left(\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{l}-\omega_{m}\right)x\right)}{\tan(x)}dx\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\omega_{m}}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\right)^{4}\left(4\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\right)^{N}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{m}}\sum_{\pm}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\left(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\right)^{N}}\right),

whereas, for large values of
i,j,l,m
such that some
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\longarrownot\longrightarrow\infty
, Holder’s inequality implies

\displaystyle\left|A_{ijl}^{(m)}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{i}}\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}e_{l}(x)\left(\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{m}(y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy\right)\tan^{2}(x)dx\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{i}}\tan(x)\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}(x)}{\omega_{j}}\tan(x)e_{l}(x)\left(\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{m}(y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy\right)dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{e_{i}^{\prime}}{\omega_{i}}\tan\right\|_{L^{2}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|\frac{e_{j}^{\prime}}{\omega_{j}}\tan\right\|_{L^{2}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|e_{l}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\left\|\int_{\cdot}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{m}(y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy\right\|_{L^{\infty}\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]}\lesssim\frac{\omega_{l}}{\omega_{m}}.

Finally, for large values of
i,j,l,m
and in the case where all
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{l}\pm\omega_{m}\longrightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle B_{ijl}^{(m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{i}(x)e_{j}(x)e_{l}(x)\left(\int_{x}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}e_{m}(y)\sin(y)\cos(y)dy\right)\tan^{2}(x)dx
